Title: Geometry-Aware Operator Families for Structured Representation Learning

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.33089v1

Published Time: Tue, 29 Sep 2026 01:38:14 GMT

Number of Pages: 155

Markdown Content:
# Geometry-Aware Operator Families for Structured Representation Learning 

Zuyuan Zhang 

The George Washington University 

zuyuan.zhang@gwu.edu 

Fei Xu Yu 

The George Washington University 

fxyu@gwu.edu 

Tian Lan 

The George Washington University 

tlan@gwu.edu 

## Abstract 

The geometry of latent representations governs which components should in-teract and how information should propagate, making geometry-aware operator design a fundamental ingredient of structured deep representation learning. How-ever, existing neural architectures typically rely on generic operator templates or geometry-specific constructions, creating a need for a unified framework that can derive admissible operators directly from fixed structural information while remaining adaptive to changing contexts. We introduce Geometry-Induced Oper-ator Families (GIOF), a general framework that converts fixed geometry into a structured family of propagation operators and dynamically selects an appropriate member of this family according to the current context. GIOF first transforms geometry-derived interaction channels into reusable generator bases, then combines them through a context-dependent selector and adaptive propagation scale, and finally realizes the selected operator through stable continuous-time propagation and a bottleneck residual layer. We establish theoretical guarantees covering pa-rameter compression, identifiability, stability, locality, compositional structure, and oversmoothing behavior, while controlled experiments validate these mechanisms and experiments on PEMS-BAY and METR-LA achieve the lowest mean MAE across all reported regional-outage settings, improving over the strongest retained baseline by 2.4%–8.8% at 30% missing sensors. 

## 1 Introduction 

Deep learning has achieved remarkable success by learning expressive hidden representations through standard computational building blocks, such as dense linear maps, convolutions, graph message passing, and attention [LeCun et al., 2015, Goodfellow et al., 2016, He et al., 2016, Krizhevsky et al., 2012, Vaswani et al., 2017, Kipf and Welling, 2016, Hamilton et al., 2017, Veliˇ ckovi´ c et al., 2017, Battaglia et al., 2018]. In these approaches, geometry may be encoded implicitly in learned representations or incorporated externally through regularization and architectural constraints, such as prescribed relational structure or symmetry [Gilmer et al., 2017, Cohen and Welling, 2016], rather than treated as a primitive object that determines the operators acting on representations during learning. When the latent space carries geometric structure, such as locality, directionality, hierarchy, obstruc-tion, or compatibility, a natural approach is to construct geometry-conditioned operator families and learn within those families. In contrast to soft regularization or architectural enforcement [Belkin and Niyogi, 2003, Coifman and Lafon, 2006, Bronstein et al., 2017, 2021, Chung, 1997, Grady and 

> Preprint. arXiv:2609.33089v1 [cs.LG] 27 Sep 2026

Polimeni, 2010, Lim, 2020, Hansen and Gebhart, 2020], this makes geometry an explicit determinant of the admissible operator class. Existing approaches include equivariant constructions [Cohen and Welling, 2016, Finzi et al., 2020], graph- and manifold-based convolutions [Monti et al., 2017], edge-conditioned filters [Simonovsky and Komodakis, 2017], and parameterizations of hidden-state dynamics [Chen et al., 2018]. These methods provide important geometry-aware mechanisms, but typically specialize the operator form to a particular geometry or architectural construction. Our goal is instead a common finite-dimensional framework in which a prescribed geometry generates the admissible operator family and supports explicit analysis of locality, propagation, coefficient structure, and stability. A broader comparison with related work is given in Appendix A. To this end, we introduce Geometry-Induced Operator Families (GIOF). Let c ∈ C denote the context available to a layer and H ∈ Rd×ph the latent representation over d sites. A fixed geometry descriptor 

S specifies an admissibility relation, pairwise geometric attributes, and a finite family of reusable geometry channels. Learning then uses c to select coefficients inside the operator family induced by 

S, rather than generating an unrestricted d × d matrix. In the propagation realization, the selector produces a generator L(c) ∈ L S and a scale τ (c) ≥ 0, yielding P (c) = eτ (c)L(c), which acts on the latent sites directly or on a bottleneck projection of H. Thus geometry determines which transformations are admissible, while context determines which admissible transformation is used for the current representation. This construction yields guarantees with direct operational meanings. One-step interaction operators have no off-diagonal entries outside the admissibility relation, while P (c) can transmit information only along admissible directed paths, so depth enlarges the receptive field without creating geometri-cally forbidden couplings. Because each admissible generator is Metzler and satisfies L(c)1 = 0 , the realized propagator obeys P (c) ≥ 0, P (c)1 = 1, ∥P (c)∥∞→∞ = 1 . For fixed c, propagation therefore does not amplify hidden signals, or the direct feature gradient through P (c), in the sup norm. When L and τ are differentiable in c, Proposition D.3 further gives the first-order context sensitivity 

∥DcP (c)[ u]∥∞→∞ ≤ τ (c)∥DcL(c)[ u]∥∞→∞ + ∥L(c)∥∞→∞ |Dcτ (c)[ u]| for a perturbation direc-tion u. Thus context changes modify propagation through controlled changes of the generator and propagation scale rather than through an unrestricted dense operator. Products of realized propagators retain nonnegativity, constant preservation, and descriptor-level reachability across depth. Reusable geometry channels also compress the operator degrees of freedom and yield identifiable coefficients under the stated independence conditions, while the symmetric case characterizes the complementary oversmoothing and gradient-attenuation regime at large propagation scales. For self-conditioned selectors c = c(H), additional selector-Jacobian terms appear, and the corresponding nonlinear stability conditions are treated separately in Section 5 and Appendix E.2. Together, these results turn latent geometry into an operator-level inductive bias rather than an auxiliary regularizer. Sections 2–5 construct the descriptor-induced families, establish their structural guarantees, and instantiate trainable interaction and propagation layers; Section 6 gives an idealized approximation and identifiability interpretation, with task-alignment and additional projection results deferred to Appendix G. 

## 2 Deep Representation Spaces and Geometry Descriptors 

This section introduces the fixed geometry descriptor from which all later operator families are constructed. We first represent hidden features as signals on finitely many latent sites, then specify directed admissibility and reachability, and finally encode pairwise geometry through a finite channel dictionary. The resulting masked channel matrices are the descriptor-level objects used by the operator constructions in Section 3. Auxiliary descriptor extensions and kernel-level formulations are deferred to Appendix B, while concrete architectural instantiations are collected separately in Appendix F. 

2.1 Latent sites and representation signals Definition 2.1 (Deep representation space) . A deep representation space is a finite indexed set 

X = {z1, . . . , z d} of distinct latent sites. A scalar hidden representation h : X → R is identified with h = ( h1, . . . , h d)⊤ ∈ Rd, where hi := h(zi).Vector-valued signals, coordinate realizations, and further interpretation are deferred to Appendix B.1. Throughout the paper we use the operator convention (Ah )i = Pdj=1 Aij hj , so Aij represents 2influence from the source site zj to the receiving site zi. Accordingly, the first argument of every ordered pair (zi, z j ) is the receiver and the second is the source. 

Definition 2.2 (Directed admissibility and reachability) . An admissibility relation is a binary relation 

E ⊆ X × X. We write zj → zi whenever (zi, z j ) ∈ E , and its binary mask is Mij := 1E (zi, z j ).We say that zi is reachable from zj , written zj ⇝ zi, if there exist ℓ ≥ 1 and sites zk0 , . . . , z kℓ

such that zj = zk0 → zk1 → · · · → zkℓ = zi. We write ∆X := {(zi, z i) : 1 ≤ i ≤ d} for the diagonal relation. Reachability always refers to a directed path of positive length; reflexivity is added separately when needed. Thus E specifies one-step locality, whereas ⇝ describes the influence that can arise after repeated composition. Canonical admissibility templates and the order-theoretic specialization are developed in Appendices B.2 and B.3. A controlled learnable extension that preserves a fixed hard support envelope is discussed separately in Appendix B.5. 

2.2 Pairwise geometry and the geometry descriptor Definition 2.3 (Geometry descriptor) . A geometry descriptor is a tuple S = X, E, Ψ, {ϕr }Rr=1 , R+

, where Ψ : X × X → Rq assigns geometric attributes to each or-dered receiver–source pair, and each geometry channel satisfies ϕr (zi, z j ) = gr

 Ψ( zi, z j ) for some 

gr : Rq → R. Each channel induces the masked channel matrix (Φ r )ij := 1E (zi, z j )ϕr (zi, z j ). The set R+ ⊆ { 1, . . . , R } indexes channels satisfying (Φ r )ij ≥ 0 for all i̸ = j; coefficients associated with these channels are constrained to be nonnegative in the operator families below. The descriptor therefore separates two roles: E imposes hard one-step support, while the channel dictionary parameterizes how admissible pairs interact. 

Standing regime. Unless stated otherwise, the structural theory assumes a fixed descriptor S.Context dependence in the later layer constructions acts through coefficients selected within this fixed descriptor-induced family. Context-dependent descriptors are discussed separately in Appendix B.4. 

Compression requirement. The descriptor yields genuine structural compression only when the channel dictionary is smaller than the ambient edgewise parameterization. In particular, assigning one independent indicator channel to every admissible off-diagonal edge recovers the full masked matrix space and removes the channel-compression advantage. Concrete channel constructions and their correspondence with classical architectural templates are deferred to Appendix F, where the abstract descriptor-to-operator construction is instantiated explicitly. 

2.3 Forward link to operator families 

The masked channel matrices {Φr }Rr=1 provide the finite-dimensional realization of the descriptor geometry. Section 3 uses them directly to construct interaction, generator, and propagation families. The equivalent kernel-level formulation and its algebraic identifiability properties are deferred to Appendix B.6. Because every Φr is supported on E, interaction operators inherit one-step locality (Proposition 3.2); the stronger pathwise and compositional consequences are developed in Section 4. 

## 3 Geometry-Induced Operator Families 

This section compiles the descriptor geometry of Section 2 into the hierarchy AS → L S → P S:masked channels define admissible one-step interactions, generator constraints impose nonnegative off-diagonal rates and constant preservation, and exponentiation yields finite propagation. We keep in the main text only the definitions and support facts needed by the later theory; algebraic identifiability, constrained-generator compilation, exponential identifiability, block lifting, and multiscale variants are deferred to Appendix C.

Fix a geometry descriptor S as in Definition 2.3, with masked channel matrices Φ1, . . . , ΦR.3Definition 3.1 (Geometry-induced interaction family) . The geometry-induced interaction family is

The channel term controls admissible cross-site coupling, whereas Diag( δ) supplies sitewise self-action. Explicit entrywise forms are deferred to Appendix C.1. Operator-level coefficient identifiabil-ity, including the diagonal-overlap caveat, is treated in Appendix C.2. 

Proposition 3.2 (One-step support of geometry-induced interactions) . For every A ∈ A S and i̸ = j,

(zi, z j ) /∈ E =⇒ Aij = 0 .

Thus the interaction family changes coefficients on admissible pairs but does not create new one-step couplings. We next restrict this family to generators whose exponentials define finite propagation.

3.3 Propagation families 

For later composition results, fixed matrices U ∈ Rd×p and V ∈ Rd×q define the constrained subfamily LS;U,V := {L ∈ L S : LU = 0 , V ⊤L = 0 }, where the columns of U are preserved right modes and V ⊤h are conserved linear observables. Its finite polyhedral compilation is deferred to Appendix C.3.

These families provide the analytical input for the next section. 

## 4 Structural Properties of Geometry-Induced Operators 

This section isolates the structural consequences needed later: coefficient compression, stable finite propagation, spectral attenuation of nonconstant modes, reachability-preserving locality, persistence 4under composition, and restriction relative to the ambient masked matrix space. Technical statements concerning orientation, general sensitivity, trainability caveats, and depth-dependent expressivity are deferred to Appendix D. 

4.1 Coefficient geometry Proposition 4.1 (Affine-hull dimension of the interaction family) . If {Φ1, . . . , ΦR, E 11 , . . . , E dd } is linearly independent, then dim  aff( AS) = dim  span( AS) = R + d. Without linear independence, both dimensions are at most R + d.

Thus a single interaction operator replaces free edgewise coefficients by at most R reusable channel coefficients plus d diagonal degrees of freedom. The same linear-independence condition also guarantees coefficient identifiability; see Proposition C.5 and Remark C.6. Appendix D.1 records the corresponding affine-hull interpretation. We return to the ambient-space restriction below. 

4.2 Stability and spectral attenuation of propagation 

The generator constraints convert the compressed interaction family into stable finite-time trans-port. We first state the operator-level guarantee and then quantify the attenuation induced by large propagation scales. 

Theorem 4.2 (Order preservation and sup-norm stability of finite propagation) . Let L ∈ L S, τ ≥ 0,and P = eτ L . Then P ≥ 0, P 1 = 1, ∥P ∥∞→∞ = 1 . Consequently, h ≤ g =⇒ P h ≤

P g, ∥P h ∥∞ ≤ ∥ h∥∞ for all h, g ∈ Rd.

Hence eτ L is a stable geometry-aware averaging operator under the column-signal convention. Spectral stability, Markov orientation, and general Fréchet sensitivity are recorded in Appendix D.2. 

Theorem 4.3 (Oversmoothing of nonconstant modes) . Assume L = L⊤ ∈ L S, ker( L) = span {1}. Let 0 = λ1 > λ 2 ≥ · · · ≥ λd, γ := −λ2 > 0, h := 1⊤hd 1. Then, for every 

τ ≥ 0, ∥eτ L h − h∥2 ≤ e−γτ ∥h − h∥2.

Thus the same propagation mechanism that is forward stable progressively suppresses nonconstant modes. The corresponding effect on trainability is captured next. 

Proposition 4.4 (Gradient attenuation in the symmetric propagation regime) . Under the assumptions of Theorem 4.3, for τ ≥ 0, let Pτ = eτ L . Then, for every h ∈ Rd, ∂∂τ Pτ h 2 ≤ ∥ L∥2e−γτ ∥h − h∥2.

Moreover, for every symmetric perturbation H satisfying H1 = 0 , ∥DLPτ [H]∥2→2 ≤ τ e −γτ ∥H∥2.

Hence large propagation scales can produce exponentially small gradients on nonconstant modes despite stable forward propagation. 

Sequential propagation and additional conditioning caveats are deferred to Appendix D.2. 

4.3 Pathwise locality 

Stability does not remove geometric locality: exponentiation enlarges one-step support only along admissible paths. 

Theorem 4.5 (Reachability and quantitative propagation locality) . Let L ∈ L S, τ > 0, and 

EL := {(zi, z j ) : i̸ = j, L ij > 0}. Choose ν > 0 such that ν ≥ max i(−Lii ). For i̸ = j,

(eτ L )ij > 0 if and only if there exists a directed path from zj to zi in EL. If the shortest such path has length r, then (eτ L )ij ≤ Pr {Poisson( ντ ) ≥ r}. If no such path exists, the entry is zero. 

Thus finite propagation creates no off-diagonal coupling outside descriptor-level reachability, while the Poisson tail quantifies how path length suppresses finite-time influence. The uniformization identity and its depth interpretation are recorded in Appendix D.3. 

4.4 Composition and persistent structural guarantees 

Let RE := ∆ X ∪ { (zi, z j ) : zj ⇝ zi} be the reflexive transitive closure of the admissibility relation. 5Definition 4.6 (Composable structural envelope) . For fixed U and V , define CE;U,V := P ∈ Rd×d : P ≥ 0, P 1 = 1, P U = U, V ⊤P = V ⊤, supp( P ) ⊆ R E .

Proposition 4.7 (Structural guarantees survive composition) . The set CE;U,V is nonempty, compact, convex, and closed under matrix multiplication. Moreover, for every finite sequence Q1, . . . , Q m ∈LS;U,V , eQm · · · eQ1 ∈ CE;U,V , with no commutativity assumption. 

Therefore nonnegative averaging, preserved right modes, conserved left observables, and exclusion of unreachable couplings persist across propagation depth. The envelope certifies structural properties rather than exact realizability: a product of admissible exponentials need not be a single exponential from the original family. Appendix D.4 makes this distinction explicit, showing exact closure for commuting generators and giving a concrete counterexample to closure in the general noncommuting case. For self-conditioned stacks, these guarantees hold pointwise for the selected propagators and do not by themselves imply nonexpansiveness of the complete nonlinear input–output map; selector dependence and intervening nonlinear transformations must be analyzed separately. 

4.5 Expressive restriction relative to the ambient masked space 

Let Eoff := {(zi, z j ) ∈ E : i̸ = j}, and define the ambient masked matrix space ME := 

n

A ∈

Rd×d Aij = 0 whenever i̸ = j and (zi, z j ) /∈ E 

o

. This is the correct ambient comparison class, because the point of the framework is not sparsity alone but compression from free edgewise parameters to reusable geometric channels; see also Appendix D.5, Remark D.14. The space ME is therefore a linear subspace of Rd×d with dim( ME ) = d + |E off |.

Theorem 4.8 (Strict expressive restriction via channel compression) . If R < |E off |, then AS ⊊ ME .

Hence channel reuse imposes a genuine single-layer operator restriction beyond support sparsity. This is a single-layer operator-space statement: it neither bounds the functional capacity of unrestricted self-conditioned selectors nor implies a depth-independent restriction on the algebra generated by repeated layers. These two qualifications are made precise in Appendices D.5 and D.4, respectively. The restriction should therefore be interpreted as a structured inductive bias: the model assumes fewer reusable geometric mechanisms than admissible directed edges. These structural properties provide the analytical basis for the geometry-aware layer constructions studied next. 

## 5 From Geometry to Deep Layers 

This section turns the operator hierarchy AS → L S → P S into trainable network modules. We retain two core realizations: interaction operators act as structured residual corrections, while generator operators induce stable latent propagation through matrix exponentiation. The experiments instantiate the propagation realization with the residual bottleneck defined below; the interaction adapter is retained as the corresponding one-step realization of the same geometry-induced principle. Balanced transport, multiscale extensions, and implementation details are deferred to Appendix E. 

5.1 Geometry-conditioned selectors and layer parameterization 

Let C ⊆ Rpc be the context domain and h ∈ Rd the representation acted on by the layer. The context c ∈ C may coincide with h, be derived from another representation stream, or include auxiliary information. Its dimension pc is independent of the feature width used by the matrix-valued realization below. For interaction layers, we use a continuous selector A : C → A S, A(c) = 

PRr=1 θr (c)Φ r + Diag( δ(c)) , θr (c) ≥ 0 for r ∈ R +. Thus learning selects context-dependent operators inside the descriptor-induced family rather than arbitrary matrices. Implementation details are deferred to Appendix E.1. 

5.2 Interaction layers: geometry-aware residual adapters Definition 5.1 (Geometry-aware residual adapter) . The geometry-aware residual adapter is 

RS(c, h ) := h + A(c)h, A(c) ∈ A S.

6Conditional locality. For fixed c, Proposition 3.2 gives (zi, z j ) /∈ E , i ̸ = j =⇒ Aij (c) = 0 ,

and hence DhRS(c, h ) = I + A(c) has no off-diagonal entries outside E. This is a conditional matrix-level statement: if the selector is self-conditioned, A = A(h), then DF (h)[ v] = ( I +

A(h)) v + DA (h)[ v]h, so matrix support alone does not guarantee functional locality. A precise locality condition and counterexample are deferred to Appendix E.2. 

5.3 Propagation layers: stable latent evolution Constructive generator parameterization. The interaction family permits general signed geome-try channels, whereas stable propagation requires nonnegative off-diagonal rates. Residual GIOF realization. For a matrix-valued hidden state H ∈ Rd×ph , the propagator acts along the d latent sites and is shared across feature channels. To reduce propagation cost, we first project the features to a q-dimensional bottleneck, F = HW in , Win ∈ Rph×q , F ∈ Rd×q ,

which is the columnwise extension of Definition 5.2. The bottleneck realization used in the experi-ments is detailed in Appendix H.3. For every fixed context c, P (c) ∈ P S. Therefore Theorem 4.2 and Theorem 4.5 apply pointwise: P (c) ≥ 0, P (c)1 = 1, P (c)ij = 0 if i̸ = j and zj̸ ⇝ zi. Thus propagation preserves nonnegativity and constant signals while allowing influence only through descriptor-admissible paths. When the selector is self-conditioned, the preceding guarantees remain valid pointwise for each realized propagator, but they do not automatically extend to the complete non-linear map h 7 → eQ(h)h. In particular, global order preservation, nonexpansiveness, and functional locality require additional control of selector dependence. Sufficient bi-Lipschitz and ancestor-locality conditions are given in Appendix E.2, Propositions E.4 and E.5. Across depth, Proposition 4.7 already shows that products of realized propagators remain nonnegative, row-stochastic, and supported within descriptor-level reachability. For self-conditioned stacks, this remains a statement about the realized operator product; the dependence of the selectors on intermediate representations must still be treated separately. 

Structured operator selection. Both constructions implement the same architectural principle: learning selects context-dependent operators from descriptor-induced families rather than searching over arbitrary matrices in Rd×d. Residual layers realize the selected operator as a correction, whereas propagation layers realize it as latent evolution. (a) Structured probe re-covery improves rapidly with the number of vector probes, while less structured estimators require substantially more observations. (b) Under matched geometry, the structured estimator approaches the exact projection floor, whereas increasing mismatch exposes the corresponding approximation cost. ## 6 Approximation and Learning Interpretation 

Restricting a learned operator to AS introduces a structured inductive bias. To isolate that effect, let 

B ∈ Rd×d denote an unrestricted target and define ΠAS (B) := arg min A∈A S ∥B − A∥2

F .

Theorem 6.1 (Best structured approximation) . For every B ∈ Rd×d, ΠAS (B) exists and is unique. 

This Frobenius projection is an auxiliary structural reference, not a claim that shared-parameter end-to-end training literally performs metric projection. With non-isotropic observations, the natural objective becomes data weighted; Proposition G.6 and the coefficient-space quadratic program are given in Appendix G.3 and Appendix G, respectively. Operator uniqueness should also be distinguished from recovery of its coefficients from observations. Let Q1, . . . , Q k denote the fixed geometry-channel and diagonal basis matrices and write A(α) = Pka=1 αaQa. For shared probes 

h1, . . . , h N , define Γab = 1

N

PNn=1 ⟨Qahn, Q bhn⟩.

Proposition 6.2 (Identifiability from shared-operator probes) . For the unconstrained least-squares problem 1

N

PNn=1 ∥A(α)hn − yn∥22, the coefficient vector is unique if and only if Γ ≻ 0. In particular, linear independence of {Qa}ka=1 together with the empirical covariance 1

N

PNn=1 hnh⊤

n ≻ 0 is sufficient, whereas matrix-basis independence alone is not. 

Thus algebraic identifiability and observational identifiability are distinct: the former concerns uniqueness of a coefficient representation once the operator is known, whereas the latter additionally requires informative probes. The same projection principle extends pointwise to input-dependent operator fields, while approximation bias, data-weighted recovery, and the spectral criterion for useful propagation are deferred to Appendix G.3. These results are interpretive statements about the structured hypothesis class and do not imply task-level gains without alignment between the descriptor and the data. 

## 7 Experiments 

We test geometric channel sharing beyond graph sparsity through controlled operator checks and real sensor reconstruction. Figure 1 reports the completed controlled checks of probe recovery, mismatch bias, pathwise influence, and useful versus excessive smoothing. Appendix H specifies the full protocol and reports additional numerical diagnostics.

Figure 2: Robustness to regional outages with 20% training days. Test MAE is reported under increasing fractions of missing sensors using fixed checkpoints. The 60% missing-ness setting lies outside the mask rates used during training. At 30% regional missingness, GIOF obtains the lowest mean MAE in all four reported dataset/data-budget set-tings. Relative to the strongest retained baseline in each column, the mean-MAE reduction ranges from 2.4% to 8.8%. The cost measurements show that this gain is not free: GIOF is slower than the lighter GCN/GATv2/APPNP/ECC modules, while its training and infer-ence costs are close to MoNet. We there-fore interpret the result as an accuracy– structure trade-off rather than a univer-sal computational advantage. 

Robustness to outage severity. Fig-ure 2 evaluates fixed 20%-data checkpoints under increasing regional-outage severity. GIOF achieves the lowest mean MAE across all tested missing-sensor fractions on both datasets, including the 60% setting that lies outside the mask rates used during training. This trend indicates that the advantage is not confined to the particular corruption levels seen during optimization. Independent-sensor, point-missingness, duration, graph-distance, and matched-ablation results are reported in Appendix H.5. Predictive improvements remain empirical observations and are not implied by the operator-level guarantees alone. 

## 8 Conclusion 

We introduced Geometry-Induced Operator Families (GIOF), a framework that uses fixed latent geometry to define structured operator families while allowing context-dependent adaptation within them. GIOF provides a unified connection between geometric structure, stable propagation, locality, and trainable deep layers, together with explicit theoretical guarantees. Controlled and real-data experiments further support these properties and show the practical value of geometry-induced operator design under structured missingness. 9References

## A Related Work 

Geometric deep learning and message passing. A large body of work incorporates geometry through graph or manifold structure. Spectral constructions derive filters from graph Laplacians and related harmonic structure [Chung, 1997, Belkin and Niyogi, 2003, Coifman and Lafon, 2006, Defferrard et al., 2016], while spatial graph neural networks aggregate information over prescribed neighborhoods [Kipf and Welling, 2016, Hamilton et al., 2017, Gilmer et al., 2017, Veliˇ ckovi´ c et al., 2017, Battaglia et al., 2018]. MoNet provides a general mixture-model view of local convolution on graphs and manifolds [Monti et al., 2017], and edge-conditioned convolution makes filters depend explicitly on edge attributes [Simonovsky and Komodakis, 2017]. These methods establish effective geometry-aware architectures, but the operator class is typically tied to a particular aggregation rule, kernel, or geometric domain. GIOF instead starts from a finite descriptor containing admissibility and reusable geometry channels, and treats the induced operator family itself as the object to be constructed and analyzed. 

Symmetry and equivariance. Equivariant networks constrain operators by requiring compatibility with a prescribed group action, as in group-equivariant convolution and its extensions to Lie groups [Cohen and Welling, 2016, Finzi et al., 2020]. This is closely related in spirit to GIOF because geometry restricts the hypothesis class by construction rather than through a soft penalty. The distinction is that GIOF does not assume that the latent geometry is generated by a group action: directed locality, barriers, hierarchical relations, and other asymmetric admissibility structures can be encoded directly. Equivariance can therefore appear as a special structured regime, but it is not the primitive organizing assumption. 12 Dynamic and context-conditioned operators. Dynamic filter networks generate input-dependent filters [Jia et al., 2016], edge-conditioned convolution generates filters from pairwise attributes [Simonovsky and Komodakis, 2017], and attention uses context-dependent scores to form data-adaptive mixing operators [Vaswani et al., 2017, Veliˇ ckovi´ c et al., 2017]. GIOF also permits context-dependent selection, but separates the selector from the admissible operator family : the context changes coefficients within a descriptor-induced family, while support, channel sharing, and generator constraints are imposed independently of the selector. This separation enables operator-level guarantees to hold pointwise for every realized context. It also clarifies their limitation: if the selector is itself conditioned on the hidden state, derivatives through the selector must be controlled separately from the fixed-context propagator. 

Diffusion, continuous-depth models, and higher-order geometry. Diffusion-based representation methods motivate propagation through geometry-aware generators [Coifman and Lafon, 2006, Grady and Polimeni, 2010], while neural ODEs model hidden representations through learned continuous-time dynamics [Chen et al., 2018]. GRAND specializes this viewpoint to graph neural diffusion [Chamberlain et al., 2021], and neural sheaf diffusion uses sheaf structure to address heterophily and oversmoothing [Bodnar et al., 2022]. Higher-order constructions based on simplicial complexes, Hodge Laplacians, and sheaves further enrich the underlying geometry [Ebli et al., 2020, Lim, 2020, Bodnar et al., 2021, Roddenberry et al., 2021, Hansen and Gebhart, 2020, Barbero et al., 2022]. GIOF is complementary to these choices of geometry: its generator family enforces Metzler and constant-preserving constraints before exponentiation, yielding nonnegative, stable finite propagation and reachability guarantees for any descriptor that can be compiled into the finite channel representation. 

Positioning of GIOF. The contribution is therefore not a new graph convolution, attention rule, diffusion equation, or equivariance principle in isolation. GIOF provides a common finite-dimensional descriptor-to-operator construction in which one can distinguish (i) admissible one-step interactions, (ii) feasible generators, (iii) finite propagation obtained by exponentiation, and (iv) context-dependent selection inside these fixed families. This organization makes it possible to state exact support and reachability properties, quantify the reduction from free edgewise parameters to shared geometry channels, separate algebraic from observational identifiability, and analyze stable propagation and its first-order sensitivity within one framework. The projection results in Appendix G are likewise an idealized characterization of the structured hypothesis class rather than an equivalence with end-to-end task optimization. 

## B Supplementary Material for Section 2 

This appendix supplements Section 2 with extensions that are useful for interpretation and imple-mentation but are not required for the minimal main-text construction. We follow the dependency structure of the main text: we first clarify the signal space and auxiliary coordinate realizations, then specialize directed admissibility and reachability, and finally discuss descriptor variants and the equivalent kernel-level realization. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, the standing regime remains the fixed geometry descriptor S of Definition 2.3; in particular, the hard admissibility relation and realized channel matrices are fixed when the later operator families are constructed. 

B.1 Signal-space extensions 

Remark B.1 (Scalar and vector-valued signals) . For conceptual clarity, Section 2 is formulated for scalar signals h ∈ Rd. If each site carries a c-dimensional feature vector, the representation may instead be written as H ∈ Rd×c. A site-level operator A ∈ Rd×d then acts channelwise as 

H 7 −→ AH. Under column-wise vectorization, this separable action is equivalently represented by 

Ic ⊗ A on Rdc . Hence all support and reachability statements at the site level extend immediately to this separable lift. More general architectures may also mix feature coordinates during cross-site transfer. Such mixing requires a genuine block-valued operator construction rather than only a change of notation; the corresponding lifting is developed in Appendix C.4. Thus the scalar formulation isolates the site-level geometry, while the block-valued extension supplies the additional feature-space mixing needed for ordinary multi-channel architectures. 13 Remark B.2 (What is and is not assumed) . The definition of a deep representation space does not assume that X is a grid, a graph, a group orbit, a manifold discretization, or a partially ordered set. Those are possible specializations, but none is required at the primitive level. What matters in the main text is only that X is a finite indexed site family on which hidden activations can be interpreted as signals. 

Remark B.3 (Auxiliary coordinate realization) . When concrete coordinates are required, we introduce an auxiliary map ι : X → Rm. This map is not part of the abstract identity of the latent sites: distinct sites may have identical coordinates under ι. It is used only when a particular admissibility rule or geometry channel requires an ambient coordinate realization. 

B.2 Admissibility templates 

Remark B.4 (Canonical admissibility templates) . Suppose that X is additionally equipped with an auxiliary coordinate map ι : X → Rm or with another external structure, depending on the application. Then several familiar interaction patterns arise as special choices of E:1. Local grid or CNN stencil. If r ≥ 0 is a radius, one may set 

(zi, z j ) ∈ E loc ⇐⇒ ∥ι(zi) − ι(zj )∥∞ ≤ r. 

2. Causal sequence geometry. If the sites are temporally indexed as z1, . . . , z d, one may impose 

(zi, z j ) ∈ E causal ⇐⇒ 0 ≤ i − j ≤ r. 

3. Graph neighborhood geometry. If the sites form the vertices of a directed graph G, let 

N −(zi) denote the in-neighborhood of the receiving site zi. Consistently with the receiver– source convention of Section 2, one may set 

(zi, z j ) ∈ E graph ⇐⇒ zj ∈ N −(zi),

so that an admissible graph edge carries information from zj to zi. For an undirected graph, 

N −(zi) may simply be replaced by the usual neighborhood N (zi). Self-loops may be included separately when desired. 4. Windowed or global attention geometry. If the sites are partitioned into windows 

W1, . . . , W s, one may impose 

(zi, z j ) ∈ E win ⇐⇒ zi and zj belong to the same window .

Full attention corresponds to E = X × X.5. Multiscale hierarchy. If the sites are organized across scales, one may let E contain both same-scale local edges and cross-scale parent-child edges. This yields a geometry appropri-ate for pyramid, U-Net, or feature-fusion architectures. The point is that admissibility is not tied to a single architecture. It is a common language for a broad class of direct-interaction patterns. 

B.3 Order-theoretic specialization 

The templates above specify direct admissible interactions. Since the later operator theory also depends on repeated composition, it is useful to distinguish this one-step relation from the order structure that may, in special cases, be induced by its reachability closure. 

Remark B.5 (Admissibility versus reachability) . The relation E need not be reflexive or transitive. By contrast, its reflexive-transitive closure is exactly the reachability relation together with identity. This distinction matters conceptually: admissibility describes which interactions are directly allowed, whereas reachability describes which influences become possible after repeated composition of such direct interactions. The preceding distinction leads to a genuine order structure precisely when directed cycles do not destroy antisymmetry. 14 Proposition B.6 (When directed locality does and does not define an order) . Let ⪯E denote the reflexive closure of reachability: 

zj ⪯E zi ⇐⇒ zj = zi or zj ⇝ zi.

Then: 1. if the directed graph (X, E) is acyclic, the relation ⪯E is a partial order on X;2. if (X, E) contains a directed cycle with at least two distinct vertices, then ⪯E fails to be antisymmetric and therefore is not a partial order. 

This proposition shows that partial order is only a special case. The primitive notion in the framework is directed locality, not order. Having clarified the consequences of the directed support relation, we now turn from admissibility itself to the pairwise geometric information carried by admissible receiver–source pairs. 

B.4 Pairwise geometric attributes 

Remark B.7 (Directed versus symmetric attributes) . Even when the ambient geometry is symmetric, the attribute map Ψ need not be symmetric. In particular, Ψ( zi, z j )̸ = Ψ( zj , z i) is fully allowed and is often desirable in settings with preferred directions, anisotropic transport, multiscale refinement, or causal asymmetry. 

Remark B.8 (Fixed versus context-dependent descriptors) . The main structural theory is stated for a fixed descriptor S. Thus, unless explicitly stated otherwise, E, the realized channel matrices {Φr }Rr=1 ,and the induced operator families do not vary with the layer context. A broader construction may allow the attribute map, channel values, or even the admissibility relation to depend on a context c, producing a context-dependent descriptor S(c). For each fixed c, the fixed-descriptor structural statements apply to the realized descriptor S(c). However, conclusions that require one common reachability relation, one common coefficient parameterization, or projection onto one fixed convex family require additional uniform assumptions across contexts. 

B.5 Learnable reweighting within a fixed admissibility envelope 

The preceding discussion allows descriptor components to vary, but exact locality is easiest to retain when a fixed hard support envelope is preserved. 

Remark B.9 (Soft gating inside a fixed support envelope) . Let Emax ⊆ X × X be a fixed maxi-mal admissibility relation, and let aη : X × X → R be a learnable score. Define the soft gate 

gη (zi, z j ) = σ aη (zi, z j ) and the gated channel eϕr,η (zi, z j ) = 1Emax (zi, z j ) gη (zi, z j ) ϕr (zi, z j ).

Then (zi, z j ) /∈ E max =⇒ eϕr,η (zi, z j ) = 0 exactly. Consequently, every directed path supported by the gated channels is contained in the reachability relation induced by Emax .Because a sigmoid gate is strictly positive for finite logits, this construction performs soft reweighting rather than exact edge deletion inside Emax . Exact adaptive sparsification would require an additional hard or sparsity-inducing gating mechanism. If aη is further allowed to depend on a context c or on the current representation, the resulting object should be interpreted as a context-dependent descriptor 

S(c), with the pointwise versus uniform distinction described in Remark B.8. The hard envelope is essential for the exact structural statement: without 1Emax , a generic sigmoid gate is dense and no nontrivial fixed support or reachability guarantee follows. We now return to the standing fixed-descriptor regime and record the equivalent kernel-level realiza-tion of its channel dictionary.

Proposition B.10 (Kernel-level structure and matrix realization) . The family KS is a finite-dimensional closed convex polyhedral cone. Under the identification [K]ij = K(zi, z j ), [Kθ ] = 

PRr=1 θr Φr . If the masked channels { eϕr }Rr=1 are linearly independent, then the map θ 7 → Kθ is injective, and hence the coefficient representation of every K ∈ K S is unique. 

This is a kernel-level identifiability statement: it concerns the coefficients θ before an independent diagonal self-action is introduced. It should therefore not be confused with identifiability in the interaction family AS, where the additional term Diag( δ) may overlap with diagonal components already contained in the channel matrices. A sufficient condition for uniqueness at that operator level is the stronger linear-independence condition on 

{Φ1, . . . , ΦR, E 11 , . . . , E dd },

stated in Proposition C.5.

Thus every off-support entry vanishes exactly, while all admissible entries are coupled through the shared coefficient vector θ. The corresponding operator family in Section 3 augments this kernel-generated matrix with an independent diagonal self-action. 

## C Supplementary Material for Section 3 

This appendix develops the operator-level material deferred from Section 3. Unless stated otherwise, we retain the fixed scalar single-scale descriptor S, the receiver–source matrix convention of Section 2, and the hierarchy 

AS → L S → P S.

We first make the matrix realization and its coefficient structure explicit, then record support and semigroup facts, next address generator feasibility, normalization, and exponential aliasing, and finally lift the construction to vector-valued and multiscale representations. These results supplement the minimal main-text chain without redefining its basic objects. 

C.1 Matrix-level remarks and explicit forms 

Remark C.1 (No new structure is introduced at the matrix level) . The matrix viewpoint adds no new geometric degrees of freedom beyond the masked-channel construction induced by the descriptor in Section 2 and its kernel-level realization in Appendix B.6. It only realizes those admissible pairwise interaction laws as linear maps on signals h ∈ Rd. Thus all operator constraints below are imposed on matrices generated by the fixed descriptor rather than on arbitrary matrices detached from latent geometry. With this scope fixed, the matrix entries can be written directly in terms of the masked geometry channels and the free diagonal self-action.

> rθr1E(z2, z d)ϕr(z2, z d)
> ....... . ....P
> rθr1E(zd, z 1)ϕr(zd, z 1)P
> rθr1E(zd, z 2)ϕr(zd, z 2)· · · δd+P
> rθr1E(zd, z d)ϕr(zd, z d)
> 

This explicit form makes clear which entries are forced to vanish by admissibility and which entries remain learnable through channel coefficients and diagonal self-action. 

Remark C.3 (Operator-level realizations of classical layers) . The interaction family contains the site-level linear skeleton of several familiar architectures: 1. Residual convolution. If X is a grid, E is a finite stencil, and the geometry channels are indexed by relative offsets, then I + A with A ∈ A S gives a geometry-aware residual convolutional coupling. Translation sharing is obtained when the channels depend only on relative offset. 2. Graph message passing. If E is graph adjacency and the channels encode edge type, direction, or metric relation, then A ∈ A S defines a graph-structured message-passing operator. 3. Attention-style interaction. With global or windowed admissibility, fixed pairwise score channels can be represented inside AS. State-dependent pair scores instead belong to the context-dependent descriptor extension discussed in Appendix B.4. Standard self-attention additionally applies a row-normalization such as softmax, so the present family should be interpreted as its geometry-aware linear score or mixing skeleton rather than as an exact identification with every attention operator. 4. Additive multiscale fusion. After the multiscale lifting of Appendix C.5, sums of transferred operators provide the linear geometric skeleton of additive cross-resolution fusion used in pyramid and encoder–decoder architectures. These examples clarify architectural scope; they introduce no additional operator theory. 

C.2 Deferred algebraic facts from Section 3 

This subsection records algebraic and support properties that are useful for later arguments but not needed in the shortest main-text chain 

AS → L S → P S.

We first describe the interaction cone and its coordinate identifiability, then pass to the generator cone, whose row-sum constraint determines the diagonal and whose powers can propagate support only along admissible paths. We close with the fixed-generator semigroup law. 

Proposition C.4 (Basic structure of the interaction family) . The set AS is a finite-dimensional closed polyhedral convex cone in Rd×d. If R+ = ∅, then AS is a linear subspace. More generally, it is generated by the finite collection {Φr : r ∈ R +} ∪ {± Φr : r / ∈ R +} ∪ {± E11 , . . . , ±Edd }, where 

Eii denotes the standard diagonal basis matrix. 

The cone representation above need not provide unique coefficients. The next statement gives a sufficient condition for uniqueness. 

Proposition C.5 (Coefficient identifiability at the operator level) . Assume that the family of matrices 

{Φ1, . . . , ΦR, E 11 , . . . , E dd } is linearly independent in Rd×d. Then every operator A ∈ A S admits a unique representation A = PRr=1 θr Φr + Diag( δ), subject to the sign constraints θr ≥ 0 for 

r ∈ R +.

The defining row-sum constraint immediately determines the generator diagonal. 

Remark C.6 (Diagonal overlap between channels and free self-action) . The independence hypothesis in Proposition C.5 is substantive. Indeed, both the channel matrices Φr and Diag( δ) may contribute to diagonal entries. If one wants identifiability to be built into the parameterization more directly, one may either impose ϕr (zi, z i) = 0 for all i, r, so that all diagonal self-action is carried by δ,or absorb each channel-generated diagonal contribution into the free diagonal term and work with off-diagonal channel realizations. The paper retains the more general formulation, so uniqueness should always be understood as conditional on the stated linear-independence assumption. 17 Having fixed the interaction-level algebra, we now restrict to the generator subclass used by finite propagation. 

Proposition C.7 (Basic structure of the generator family) . The set LS is a closed polyhedral convex cone in Rd×d. Equivalently, LS = AS ∩

Proposition C.8 (Diagonal structure of generators) . Every L ∈ L S has nonpositive diagonal entries, and for each i, Lii = − P  

> j̸=i

Lij . In particular, the diagonal of a generator is completely determined by its off-diagonal entries. 

Membership in AS simultaneously preserves the one-step admissibility pattern. 

Proposition C.9 (Support of generator off-diagonal entries) . If L ∈ L S, then for all i̸ = j, Lij ̸ =0 = ⇒ (zi, z j ) ∈ E . Thus, even before exponentiation, a generator already respects the one-step admissibility pattern of the underlying geometry descriptor. 

Repeated multiplication can enlarge support only by concatenating such admissible one-step moves. 

Proposition C.10 (Pathwise support of powers) . Let L ∈ L S, and let ¯E := E ∪ ∆X , ∆X := 

{(zi, z i) : 1 ≤ i ≤ d}. Then for every n ≥ 1, the support of Ln is contained in ¯E◦n. In particular, if i̸ = j and there is no directed admissible path from zj to zi, that is, zj̸ ⇝ zi, then (Ln)ij =0 for every n ≥ 1.

The exponential aggregates these admissible powers. For a fixed generator it also has the standard semigroup structure. 

Proposition C.11 (Semigroup property) . Let L ∈ L S. Then the family {eτ L }τ ≥0 forms a one-parameter semigroup: e0·L = I, e(τ1+τ2)L = eτ1Leτ2L for all τ1, τ 2 ≥ 0.

The preceding semigroup identity concerns repeated propagation with a fixed generator. Composition across distinct generators is treated in Appendix D.4; in particular, the full set-valued propagation family is generally not closed under multiplication. For residual interaction operators, however, the corresponding pathwise support statement is immediate and useful on its own. 

Proposition C.12 (Pathwise support of repeated residual interactions) . If A(1) , . . . , A (m) ∈ A S,then 

supp



⊆ ¯E◦m, ¯E := E ∪ ∆X .

Thus depth enlarges the receptive field only through concatenations of admissible one-step interac-tions. 

C.3 Feasibility, constrained compilation, and exponential identifiability 

The previous subsection describes intrinsic algebraic structure. We now ask three constructive questions: when the generator cone is nontrivial, how a linearly constrained generator family can be compiled into a finite normalized dictionary, and what normalization does—and does not—identify after matrix exponentiation. 

Proposition C.13 (A sufficient condition for nontrivial generator feasibility) . If some r0 ∈ R +

satisfies (Φ r0 )ij > 0 for at least one i̸ = j, then

18 Consequently, nontrivial feasibility of the constrained cone can be checked by its linear constraints together with ρ(L) = 1 . Enumerating all vertices recovers the exact normalized polytope, whereas any smaller feasible dictionary defines a certified subfamily. A finite-logit softmax reaches only the relative interior of the simplex; exact boundary selection requires a map into the closed simplex, such as Euclidean projection. In either case, the mixture coefficients are identifiable only under additional affine-independence assumptions on the chosen dictionary. 

Remark C.15 (Generator normalization does not remove exponential aliasing) . The unique scale– shape decomposition of a known generator does not imply identifiability from its exponential. Let

C.4 Block-valued lifting 

In realistic deep models, each latent site typically carries a feature vector rather than a scalar. The scalar construction must therefore be lifted to vector-valued signals. Let each site carry a c-dimensional feature vector, so that the total representation space becomes

Remark C.20 (Block lifting and positivity) . Once block lifting has been performed, positivity and conservation are conditions on the full lifted matrix, not merely on its d × d site-level skeleton. Accordingly, Definition C.19 is appropriate only when the ordered positive cone on the feature space is semantically meaningful. For arbitrary sign-indefinite hidden features, the block-valued interaction family is usually the relevant object, not the block-valued generator family. 

Remark C.21 (Block lifting and classical architectures) . Block lifting is the point at which the framework reaches ordinary multi-channel deep representations. For example, a width-preserving multi-channel convolution can be represented by a local site geometry together with offset-dependent feature-mixing blocks. Likewise, graph neural layers with learnable feature transforms are naturally represented by graph-structured Φr tensored with channel-mixing blocks Gs. Layers that change feature width require the corresponding rectangular input–output block extension rather than the square lift used here. 

C.5 Multiscale variants 

The block lift changes the feature space carried by each site, whereas the multiscale lift changes the resolution of the site space itself; these two extensions are logically independent. Many deep representations exhibit coarse-to-fine structure, nested neighborhoods, or hierarchical aggregation, which motivates the following transferred-operator construction. Assume that, in addition to the finest latent space X(0) = X, we are given coarser latent spaces 

X(1) , . . . , X (L), with dℓ = |X(ℓ)|, and fixed transfer operators Pℓ : Rdℓ → Rd0 , Rℓ : Rd0 →

Rdℓ , ℓ = 0 , . . . , L, where P0 = R0 = Id0 . Each scale X(ℓ) carries its own geometry descriptor 

S(ℓ), and hence its own interaction family AS(ℓ) and generator family LS(ℓ) .

Definition C.22 (Multiscale interaction family) . The multiscale interaction family is

Proposition C.24 shows that constant preservation transfers cleanly under a natural compatibility con-dition on the restriction operators. What may still fail after transfer is the off-diagonal nonnegativity required of a genuine finest-space generator.

Proposition C.26 (Basic structure of multiscale families) . Assume that the transfer operators Pℓ and

More precisely, Ams and bLms are linear images of finite-dimensional polyhedral cones, while Lms is obtained from bLms by imposing additional linear equations and coordinatewise inequalities. 

For every nonzero L ∈ L ms , off-diagonal nonnegativity and L1d0 = 0 imply ρms (L) > 0. Hence whenever Lms ̸ = {0}, every nonzero multiscale generator has a unique rate–shape decomposition and the normalized slice below is nonempty.

This is the direct multiscale analogue of Definition 3.5: the normalized generator specifies propagation shape on the finest realized space, while τ controls its overall magnitude. 

Remark C.28 (Effective geometry in the multiscale case) . In the single-scale setting, propagation support is controlled directly by reachability in the admissibility graph (X, E). In the multiscale setting, the effective finest-space support is determined not only by the scale-wise admissibility relations but also by the transfer operators Pℓ and Rℓ. For that reason, the relevant support analysis in the multiscale case must be carried out at the level of the realized finest-space operator L ∈ L ms ,rather than by reading off reachability from any one scale in isolation. 

Remark C.29 (Multiscale operators as geometric realizations of pyramid architectures) . The mul-tiscale interaction family provides the operator-level template behind architectures that repeatedly exchange information across resolutions. Fine-scale descriptors model local detail and short-range anisotropy. Coarse-scale descriptors model long-range organization and large receptive fields. Trans-fer operators move these effects across scales. In this sense, the multiscale family is the natural geometry-induced abstraction of pyramid, encoder-decoder, and U-Net/FPN-style feature interaction. 

## D Supplementary Material for Section 4 

This appendix supplements the structural results of Section 4 in the same order as the main-text argument. We first clarify the coefficient-geometry interpretation of the interaction family, then record dynamic and spectral consequences of generator propagation, give a uniformization-based interpretation of pathwise locality, distinguish fixed-generator and commuting composition from the general noncommuting case, and finally clarify the scope of the single-layer ambient-space restriction. Throughout, we use without restatement the operator-level facts established in Appendix C. In particular, the polyhedral structure of AS and LS, coefficient identifiability, one-step and pathwise support, and the fixed-generator semigroup law are given by Propositions C.4, C.7, C.5, 3.2, C.10, and C.11, respectively. The results below therefore focus only on consequences and qualifications specific to the structural properties developed in Section 4. 

D.1 Coefficient-geometry remarks 

The dimension statement in Proposition 4.1 concerns the dimension of the realized operator family, whereas uniqueness of its coefficient representation is a separate algebraic issue. The latter is governed by Proposition C.5; in particular, the possible overlap between channel-generated diagonal entries and the free diagonal term is discussed in Remark C.6. We therefore only record here the affine-geometric point needed to interpret the main-text dimension statement. 21 Remark D.1 (Affine hull versus linear span) . Because AS is a cone containing the origin, its affine hull is a linear subspace and therefore coincides with its linear span: 

aff( AS) = span( AS).

Hence the two dimension expressions used in Proposition 4.1 are equivalent. 

D.2 Dynamic and spectral remarks 

Theorem 4.2 establishes the basic forward stability of eτ L . The following results clarify, in turn, its Markov orientation, sensitivity to the generator and propagation magnitude, spectral location, behavior under sequential symmetric propagation, and the distinction between forward stability and backward conditioning. 

Remark D.2 (Operator orientation and mass conservation) . Under the column-signal convention 

h 7 −→ eτ L h, 

the condition L1 = 0 implies eτ L 1 = 1. Thus eτ L preserves constant observables and is naturally interpreted as a backward Markov operator. For a column probability vector, the corresponding forward evolution is p(τ ) = eτ L ⊤

p(0) . Consequently, applying eτ L itself to column mass vectors preserves total mass only under the additional balance condition 1⊤L = 0 .

The forward stability theorem also controls first-order perturbations of the propagator. 

Proposition D.3 (Fréchet sensitivity of generator propagation) . For 

∥M ∥∞→∞ := max

The same generator constraints also place the spectrum in the closed left half-plane. 

Proposition D.4 (Spectral stability of generators) . For every L ∈ L S, Re( λ) ≤ 0 for every λ ∈

σ(L), and 0 ∈ σ(L).

The preceding conclusions depend essentially on the generator constraints and should not be trans-ferred to arbitrary residual interaction operators. 

Remark D.5 (Residual interactions versus stable propagation) . Membership in AS alone does not imply the positivity, constant-preservation, spectral, or contraction properties of Theorem 4.2. Those conclusions are specific to LS and to propagation operators obtained by exponentiating its elements. Thus the two operator families play different roles: AS describes structured residual correction, whereas LS describes admissible infinitesimal transport. For symmetric generators, the single-generator oversmoothing estimate extends multiplicatively to a sequence of possibly different generators. 

Proposition D.6 (Sequential symmetric oversmoothing) . Let

Forward contraction should nevertheless be distinguished from conditioning of the backward map. 22 Remark D.7 (Forward stability, invertibility, and trainability) . Suppose L = L⊤ has eigenvalues 

−λ1, . . . , −λd, 0 = λ1 ≤ · · · ≤ λd.

Then the singular-value multipliers of eτ L are e−τ λ k , and therefore 

κ2(eτ L ) = eτ λ d = eτ λ max (−L).

Thus forward nonexpansiveness does not imply a well-conditioned inverse or well-conditioned backward propagation. At the opposite endpoint, DLeτ L [H] τ =0 = 0 , so exact identity initialization produces zero first-order sensitivity with respect to generator-shape parameters when the propagation magnitude is initialized at τ = 0 . Finally, the normalization ρ(L) = 1 removes the scalar generator–magnitude ambiguity, but does not by itself bound local rates, spectral conditioning, or derivatives of a context-dependent selector. 

D.3 Locality and finite-propagation interpretation 

The reachability statement in Theorem 4.5 can be understood through uniformization: finite-time propagation is a nonnegative mixture of discrete path lengths rather than an unrestricted dense interaction. 

Proposition D.8 (Uniformization identity for finite propagation) . Let L ∈ L S, τ > 0, and choose 

ν > 0 such that ν ≥ max i(−Lii ). Define T := I + Lν . Then 

eτ L = e−ντ 

> ∞

X

> k=0

(ντ )k

k! T k.

Moreover, T ≥ 0, T 1 = 1. If EL := {(zi, z j ) : i̸ = j, L ij > 0}, then the off-diagonal support of T is contained in EL, while its diagonal entries represent holding at the current site. Hence the exponential is a Poisson mixture of admissible discrete path lengths. 

This identity makes explicit why exponentiation enlarges one-step support only through path concate-nation. 

Remark D.9 (Why geometry persists across depth) . Suppose that each layer selects a generator 

Lk ∈ L S from the same fixed descriptor S. Each Lk has off-diagonal support contained in the one-step admissibility relation E, while products and exponentials can create new couplings only by concatenating such admissible edges. Consequently, increasing propagation time or composing multiple layers may enlarge the effective receptive field, but only inside the reflexive transitive closure of E. Long-range coupling therefore remains geometrically organized rather than becoming an arbitrary dense interaction. The same observation also clarifies the range of feature vectors obtainable from a propagation operator. 

Remark D.10 (Averaging is not unrestricted transport) . For each site i, define 

Si := {j : j = i or zj ⇝ zi}.

Let P = eτ L , L ∈ L S, H ∈ Rd×c. By positivity, row-stochasticity, and reachability preservation, 

(P H )i ∈ conv {Hj : j ∈ Si}.

Thus finite propagation performs reachability-constrained convex aggregation. Moreover, 

det( eτ L ) = eτ tr( L) > 0.

Hence the propagation family does not contain every row-stochastic matrix. For example, the two-site swap matrix has determinant −1 and therefore cannot equal eτ L for any real generator L.23 D.4 Composition and depth-dependent algebra 

Proposition C.11 gives exact closure when a single generator is reused. For distinct generators, it is useful to separate the commuting case, where a single-generator representation survives, from the general noncommuting case, where the structural envelope of Proposition 4.7 remains valid even though exact membership in PS may fail. 

Proposition D.11 (Composition of commuting propagations) . Let

Thus commuting propagations are closed after the same shape–magnitude normalization used in Definition 3.5. Without commutativity, however, such a representation is not guaranteed.

The failure of single-exponential closure should not be confused with the algebraic expressivity obtainable from repeated channel composition. We next make this distinction explicit. 

Proposition D.13 (Single-layer compression need not persist algebraically) . Define the effective channel relation 

EΦ := {(zi, z j ) : i̸ = j, (Φ r )ij ̸ = 0 for some r} ,

24 where Alg R denotes the smallest unital real matrix algebra containing the displayed generators. Indeed, 

Eii Φr Ejj = (Φ r )ij Eij ,

so every effective one-step edge matrix unit can be isolated, while products of matrix units generate exactly the pairs connected by admissible paths. In particular, if the effective channel graph is strongly connected, then 

Alg R (I, Φ1, . . . , ΦR, E 11 , . . . , E dd ) = Rd×d.

This is an algebraic arbitrary-depth statement and does not imply that every matrix is representable by any fixed-depth architecture. 

D.5 Scope of the ambient-space restriction 

The strict-inclusion result in Theorem 4.8 is a statement about the space of realized single-layer matrices. Two qualifications are useful: the correct ambient comparison is the masked matrix space rather than the full matrix space, and the dimension of the selected matrix family does not by itself control the functional capacity of a context-dependent selector. 

Remark D.14 (Why the correct ambient comparison is ME ). The relevant comparison class for AS

is not the unrestricted space Rd×d, but 

ME =

n

A ∈ Rd×d Aij = 0 whenever i̸ = j and (zi, z j ) /∈ E 

o

.

Every geometry-induced interaction already obeys the same hard support mask. The distinctive restriction of AS is therefore not sparsity alone, but the further compression from independent admissible-edge coefficients to a smaller set of reusable geometry-channel coefficients. The preceding operator-space restriction should not be interpreted as a capacity bound for unrestricted self-conditioned selectors. 

Proposition D.15 (Operator dimension does not bound selector capacity) . Let K = [1 , 2] d. For every continuous map f : K → Rd, there exists a continuous selector A : K → A S using only diagonal matrices such that h + A(h)h = f (h) for every h ∈ K. 

A concrete selector is 

A(h) = Diag 

 f1(h)

h1

− 1, . . . , fd(h)

hd

− 1



.

Because hi ∈ [1 , 2] , this selector is continuous; because arbitrary diagonal self-action belongs to AS,it is admissible; and direct substitution gives 

h + A(h)h = f (h).

Remark D.16 (Scope of the dimensional restriction) . The dimensional restriction of AS concerns the family of matrices that can be selected at a single input, not the statistical or functional complexity of a self-conditioned map. Functional expressivity, sample complexity, and generalization additionally depend on the selector architecture, parameter sharing, regularity, depth, and the permitted diago-nal corrections. Proposition D.15 concerns unrestricted continuous selectors and does not assert universality for any fixed finite-capacity implementation. Together with Proposition D.13, these observations separate three distinct notions of expressivity: single-layer operator-space dimension, arbitrary-depth algebraic closure, and the functional capacity of a context-dependent selector. 

## E Supplementary Material for Section 5 

This appendix supplements Section 5 by separating four issues that are deliberately compressed in the main text. First, we clarify how geometry-conditioned selectors are implemented and distin-guish fixed-context operator guarantees from properties of fully self-conditioned maps. Second, we record the additional regularity and locality conditions required when the selector depends on the representation being transformed. Third, we develop two extensions of the basic layer construc-tion: balanced transport, which additionally preserves total mass, and multiscale correction, which combines geometry-aware interactions across resolutions. Finally, we summarize the resulting layer implementations algorithmically. Throughout, the operator-level guarantees inherited from Sections 3 and 4 are kept separate from claims about the complete nonlinear input–output map. 25 E.1 Implementation remarks for geometry-conditioned selectors 

Remark E.1 (Selector inputs need not equal acted-on signals) . For notational clarity, Section 5 separates the context variable c ∈ C from the acted-on signal h ∈ Rd. Conceptually, however, these roles may coincide. The self-conditioned case is obtained by taking C ⊆ Rd and setting 

c = h. More generally, the selector may depend on the current hidden state, on a parallel context stream, on cross-attention features, or on any other side information available to the architecture. The mathematical structure of the layer depends only on the fact that the realized operator lies in the prescribed geometry-induced family. 

Remark E.2 (Normalized generator selection and scale separation) . When propagation is written in the scale–shape form eτ (c)L(c), the selector should produce a normalized generator shape L(c) ∈ L (1)  

> S

,

so that L(c) determines propagation geometry while τ (c) determines its overall magnitude. This is the convention used in Section 5. There are several valid implementations. One may directly parameterize coefficients subject to the defining constraints of L(1)  

> S

, project a raw matrix onto L(1)  

> S

, or form a convex combination of preconstructed normalized generator basis elements bBr ∈ L (1)  

> S

. The constructive selector introduced in Section 5, which forms L(c) as a convex combination of the normalized generator basis elements 

bBr , uses the third route. By contrast, if a selector produces an unnormalized generator Q(c) ∈ L S, then Q(c) should either be used directly through eQ(c), or, whenever Q(c)̸ = 0 , decomposed uniquely as Q(c) = 

ρ(Q(c)) bL(c), bL(c) ∈ L (1)  

> S

. The zero generator corresponds to the identity propagation. Finally, predicting arbitrary edgewise nonnegative rates on the admissibility mask and balancing only the diagonal is not sufficient to guarantee membership in LS. Such a construction satisfies the ambient masked-generator constraints but may lie outside the compressed geometry-channel family 

AS.The preceding parameterizations also preserve the basic well-posedness of the layer maps. Because matrix multiplication and matrix exponentiation are continuous, continuous selectors A(·), L(·),and τ (·) induce jointly continuous maps (c, h ) 7 −→ h + A(c)h and (c, h ) 7 −→ eτ (c)L(c)h. Thus no additional regularity issue arises at the fixed-context layer level. The situation changes when the selector itself depends on the acted-on representation, which is treated next. 

E.2 Self-conditioned layers: locality and conditioning 

The fixed-context guarantees of Section 5 concern realized operators. When the context is itself a function of the acted-on representation, and in particular when c = h, two additional questions arise. First, selector dependence may introduce functional couplings that are not visible from the support of the realized matrix alone. Second, variation of the selected propagator may change the conditioning of the complete nonlinear map. We therefore treat residual functional locality first, propagation conditioning second, and ancestor-local functional dependence third. For propagation it is convenient to absorb scale and shape into the integrated generator 

Q(h) := τ (h)L(h).

Remark E.3 (Matrix support versus functional dependence) . For a differentiable self-conditioned adapter 

F (h) = h + A(h)h, DF (h)[ v] = ( I + A(h)) v + DA (h)[ v]h. 

Hence support restrictions on A(h) do not imply the same sparsity for the full Jacobian. A sufficient condition for one-step functional locality is that the i-th row of A(h) depend only on 

{hi} ∪ { hj : ( zi, z j ) ∈ E} .

For example, in dimension two with no admissible off-diagonal edges, A(h) = Diag( h2, 0) is admissible, but F1(h) = (1 + h2)h1, so the output still depends on h2.26 Proposition E.4 (Conditioning of self-conditioned propagation) . Let K ⊆ Rd satisfy 

sup h∈K ∥h∥∞ ≤ M . Let 

F (h) = eQ(h)h, 

where Q(h) is Metzler, Q(h)1 = 0 , and

Then  e−b − M K Q

∥h − g∥∞.

Hence F is bi-Lipschitz on K whenever M K Q < e −b.

The preceding proposition controls input–output conditioning, but it does not determine which input coordinates may influence a given output coordinate. Functional locality requires a separate restriction on how the selector depends on its input. This motivates the following ancestor-local condition. 

Proposition E.5 (Functional locality of adaptive propagation) . For each i, let 

Si := {i} ∪ { j : zj ⇝ zi}.

Suppose Q(h) ∈ L S and 

hSi = gSi =⇒ Q(h)i, : = Q(g)i, :.

Then, for F (h) = eQ(h)h,

hSi = gSi =⇒ Fi(h) = Fi(g).

The same property is preserved under compositions satisfying the same ancestor-local dependence condition. 

To see why the rowwise condition is sufficient, note that j ∈ Si implies Sj ⊆ Si. Hence hSi = gSi

forces all generator rows that can participate in a directed path ending at i to agree for h and g.Because every term in the i-th row of Qk is assembled from such ancestor rows, the same is true for the power series defining eQ. Thus the i-th propagated output can depend only on coordinates in Si.The condition applies to the integrated generator Q(h) = τ (h)L(h), not merely to the support of 

L(h). Hence a globally input-dependent scale or coefficient head need not satisfy functional locality without additional parameter sharing.

E.3 Depth-consistent propagation 

The pathwise interpretation of geometry-aware propagation is meaningful only when subsequent operations respect the distinction between latent-site mixing and feature-channel mixing. An unre-stricted operator that mixes the latent-site index can introduce dependencies outside descriptor-level reachability and thereby destroy the pathwise interpretation. By contrast, feature-channel transforma-tions applied independently at every site—for example, right multiplication of H ∈ Rd×ph by Win 

or Wout —do not by themselves create new site-to-site paths. Accordingly, the residual bottleneck in Eq. equation 1 is compatible with the propagation interpre-tation: the geometry-aware operator acts along the latent-site dimension, while Win and Wout act only along feature channels. Across multiple geometry-aware layers, Proposition 4.7 then controls the support of the realized propagation product, provided that the intervening site-to-site operators obey the same descriptor-level reachability envelope. For self-conditioned stacks, this remains an operator-level statement; the dependence of each selector on intermediate representations must additionally satisfy conditions such as those in Proposition E.5. 

Remark E.6 (Why one geometry-aware layer is often not enough) . If geometry is imposed only at a single layer and then followed by unconstrained dense mixing, the pathwise interpretation is immediately lost. By contrast, when the same descriptor or a compatible family of descriptors is preserved across depth, long-range feature transport remains mediated by admissible paths. This is the architectural meaning of geometry-aware propagation: it is a depth-consistent transport bias, not merely a one-off local regularizer. 

E.4 Balanced transport layers 

The propagation family of Section 5 preserves nonnegativity and constant signals, but these properties do not imply conservation of the sum of the entries of a column-vector signal. When the latent dynamics are intended to represent redistribution of nonnegative mass, we therefore impose an additional left-conservation constraint. Under the column-vector convention used throughout the paper, total-mass conservation means 

1⊤y = 1⊤h. For an infinitesimal generator this requires 1⊤L = 0 , in addition to the constant-preservation condition L1 = 0 .

Definition E.7 (Balanced generator family) . The balanced generator family associated with S is 

Lbal  

> S

:= L ∈ L S 1⊤L = 0 .

Thus a balanced generator is simultaneously Metzler, constant preserving, and total-mass preserving. In the constrained-family notation introduced in Section 3, Lbal  

> S

= LS;1,1.

To retain the same scale–shape convention as the main propagation family, we also normalize nonzero balanced generators. 

Definition E.8 (Normalized balanced generator shape) . If Lbal 

> S

̸ = {0}, define 

Lbal ,(1)  

> S

:= L ∈ L bal  

> S

ρ(L) = 1 .

28 Every nonzero balanced generator has ρ(L) > 0, so the normalized balanced slice is nonempty whenever the balanced family is nontrivial. This normalization keeps the generator L responsible for propagation shape and reserves the scalar τ for propagation magnitude, exactly as in Definition 3.5. 

Proposition E.9 (Acyclicity obstructs nontrivial balance) . Suppose that the directed graph induced by the admissible off-diagonal relation Eoff is acyclic. Then Lbal  

> S

= {0}. Hence every balanced propagation associated with this descriptor is the identity. More generally, every positive off-diagonal edge of a balanced generator belongs to a directed cycle of its positive-rate graph. 

A directed cycle in the admissibility graph is therefore necessary for Lbal 

> S

̸ = {0}. It is not sufficient: because AS may be a strict compressed subset of the ambient masked matrix space, the channel-sharing constraints may exclude every balanced circulation supported on that cycle. Thus graph-theoretic feasibility and channel-family feasibility are distinct requirements. 

Definition E.10 (Geometry-aware balanced transport layer) . Assume Lbal 

> S

̸ = {0}. Let L : C → Lbal ,(1)  

> S

and τ : C → [0 , ∞) be continuous. A geometry-aware balanced transport layer is the map 

TS : C × Rd → Rd defined by TS(c, h ) = T (c)h, T (c) := eτ (c)L(c).

The word “transport” now has a precise meaning. The off-diagonal coefficients of L(c) describe directed transfers between sites, L(c)1 = 0 preserves uniform levels, and the additional condition 

1⊤L(c) = 0 enforces conservation of total mass. 

Proposition E.11 (Mass-conservation interpretation) . Let TS be a geometry-aware balanced trans-port layer and write T (c) = eτ (c)L(c). Then, for every c ∈ C ,1. T (c)ij ≥ 0 for all i, j ;2. T (c)1 = 1;3. 1⊤T (c) = 1⊤;4. if h ≥ 0, then T (c)h ≥ 0;5. for every h ∈ Rd, 1⊤T (c)h = 1⊤h. 

Hence T (c) is doubly stochastic. For nonnegative inputs, the layer therefore redistributes signal mass without creating negative values or changing total mass. In particular, if h ≥ 0, 1⊤h = 1 , then 

TS(c, h ) remains in the probability simplex. 

In particular, if h ≥ 0 and 1⊤h = 1 , then the output remains in the probability simplex. 

Remark E.12 (Propagation versus transport) . Every balanced transport layer is a propagation layer, but not every propagation layer is a transport layer. The term “transport” should be reserved for settings in which positivity and mass conservation are part of the intended semantic interpretation of the latent dynamics. 

Proposition E.13 (Balanced transport under global average pooling) . Let 

H ∈ Rd×p, g(H) := 1

and let T (H) ∈ Rd×d be doubly stochastic for every H. Then 

g(T (H)H) = g(H),

even when T depends on H.More generally, for arbitrary compatible feature maps Win ∈ Rp×q , Wout ∈ Rq×p, the residual bottleneck eH = H + T (H) − Id

also satisfies g( eH) = g(H). Consequently, if a balanced transport branch is followed immediately by global average pooling, its contribution to the pooled representation is identically zero. Parameters used only to construct T (H) therefore receive zero gradient through that pooled path.

Operationally, each scale first restricts the finest representation, applies a geometry-aware interaction at that resolution, and transfers the result back to the finest space. Therefore propagation across scales must use the actual multiscale generator family Lms of Definition C.25, rather than exponentiating an arbitrary transferred generator sum. The multiscale realization is useful when relevant dependencies occur at widely separated spatial or structural ranges: fine scales retain local geometric detail, whereas coarse scales provide shorter routes for long-range interaction.
