There is no family of independently tunable pairwise coefficients αij whose values could generate arbitrary matrix asymmetry. Rather, the entries are tied through the same anisotropic drift formula. If the admissibility relation itself is directed, the mask can provide an additional geometry-induced source of asymmetry. Indeed, it may happen that (zi, z j ) ∈ E but (zj , z i) /∈ E . Then 

(Kdrift )ij = κaniso (zi, z j ) > 0, whereas (Kdrift )ji = 0 . This asymmetry is again imposed by the geometry descriptor, now through its directed admissibility relation, rather than through unconstrained matrix parameters. In particular, when the admissibility relation is symmetric, any remaining asymmetry of the channel values is generated entirely by the receiver-dependent anisotropic metrics and drift vectors described above. Therefore Kdrift is a geometry-induced interaction operator, and its possible directional asymmetry is structurally prescribed by the geometry descriptor—in particular by the receiver-dependent Gi

and bi, together with any directionality already encoded by E—rather than by arbitrary free matrix entries.

Even if D is symmetric, no fixed definiteness follows without a sign condition on D. For example, choose D = βI c with β > 0. Then I|V | ⊗ D = βI |V |c, and hence

It remains to clarify why these spectral conclusions do not imply a coordinatewise positivity-preserving interpretation after flattening the stalk coordinates. Positive or negative semidefiniteness constrains the quadratic form h⊤Ash h, but it does not impose a sign on individual off-diagonal entries of the flattened matrix. This distinction can be seen directly from the block structure of the sheaf Laplacian. Consider one oriented edge e : u → v. The contribution of this edge to the coboundary has the two nonzero vertex blocks −Rue and Rve . Consequently, the corresponding contribution to (δF 

> 0

> 0

> ue

1 00 −1

R⊤

> ue

1 00 −1

Hence the u, v off-diagonal block of −α∆sh contains the entry −α < 0 in its second feature coordinate. Since u̸ = v, this is an off-diagonal entry of the fully flattened 2|V | × 2|V | matrix. Therefore −α∆sh is symmetric negative semidefinite in this example but is not Metzler. The same example also shows why negative semidefiniteness alone does not imply positivity preser-vation of the exponential flow. Let A = −α∆sh for the preceding construction, and let (r, s ) denote the flattened off-diagonal coordinate for which Ars < 0. The matrix exponential has the expansion 

etA = I + tA + O(t2) as t ↓ 0.

Because r̸ = s, Irs = 0 , and therefore 

(etA )rs = tA rs + O(t2).

Since Ars < 0, the linear term is strictly negative. Hence, for all sufficiently small t > 0, (etA )rs < 0.

Thus the exponential is not entrywise nonnegative in general, despite the fact that A is symmetric negative semidefinite. Accordingly, symmetry and spectral definiteness are logically distinct from the ordered-coordinate conditions required for a block-valued Markov generator. To invoke the positivity-preserving interpre-tation of Definition C.19, one must separately verify the flattened conditions Lαβ ≥ 0 for all α̸ =

β, together with L1dc = 0 . Those properties do not follow merely from the sheaf-Laplacian Gram structure or from negative semidefiniteness. This proves all claims of the proposition.

I.52 Proof of Proposition G.2

Since the choice of A ∈ A S was arbitrary, the variational inequality holds for every feasible structured operator. Suppose now that R+ = ∅. By Proposition C.4, AS is then a linear subspace of Rd×d. In particular, 

A⋆ ∈ A S. Let A ∈ A S be arbitrary. Since a linear subspace is closed under addition and scalar multiplication, A⋆ + A ∈ A S and A⋆ − A ∈ A S.

Combining the two inequalities ⟨B − A⋆, A ⟩F ≤ 0 and ⟨B − A⋆, A ⟩F ≥ 0 forces 

⟨B − A⋆, A ⟩F = 0 .

Thus, in the unconstrained case, the residual B −A⋆ belongs to the Frobenius-orthogonal complement of the structured linear subspace: B − A⋆ ∈ A ⊥

> S

. Equivalently, B = A⋆ + ( B − A⋆) is the orthogonal decomposition of B into its structured component A⋆ ∈ A S and an orthogonal residual. This proves both statements of the proposition.

> C∈A S

> F

= dist  B, AS

2 is the smallest squared approximation error attainable by any operator in the prescribed structured family. In this precise sense, it is the irreducible structured approximation error associated with the descriptor. For any other structured operator A ∈ A S, the quantity ∥A − A⋆∥2 

> F

measures its squared Frobenius deviation from the best structured target. Indeed, subtracting the optimal approximation error from the exact expansion gives

> F

− ∥ B − A⋆∥2 

> F

= ∥A − A⋆∥2

> F

− 2 ⟨B − A⋆, A − A⋆⟩F .

> F

> F

> F

. Thus, in the general constrained convex case, deviation from A⋆ provides a lower bound on the excess approximation error. In the unconstrained linear-subspace case, the projection residual is orthogonal to the structured subspace, the cross term vanishes, and this lower bound becomes the exact Pythagorean decomposition.

I.56 Proof of Theorem G.8 

Proof. For notational convenience, write C := AS ⊆ Rd×d, and let P := Π C denote the Frobenius metric projection onto C. By Proposition C.4, C is a nonempty closed convex subset of Rd×d. In particular, the zero matrix belongs to C, since it is obtained in Definition 3.1 by taking θ = 0 , δ =0. Moreover, by Proposition G.1, the projection map P is 1-Lipschitz with respect to the Frobenius norm: 

∥P (M1) − P (M2)∥F ≤ ∥ M1 − M2∥F for all M1, M 2 ∈ Rd×d.

We first establish the basic geometric properties of FS. Recall that 

FS = A ∈ L2 K, μ ; Rd×d A(x) ∈ C for μ-almost every x .

A0 ∈ F S, and therefore FS̸ = ∅.

We next prove convexity. Let A1, A 2 ∈ F S, t ∈ [0 , 1] . Since A1, A 2 ∈ L2, their convex combination At := (1 − t)A1 + tA 2 also belongs to L2 K, μ ; Rd×d . By the definition of FS, there exist μ-null sets N1, N 2 ⊆ K such that A1(x) ∈ C for x ∈ K \ N1 and A2(x) ∈ C for x ∈

K \ N2. The union N := N1 ∪ N2 is again a μ-null set. For every x ∈ K \ N, both A1(x) and 

A2(x) belong to the convex set C. Consequently, 

At(x) = (1 − t)A1(x) + tA 2(x) ∈ C .

Thus At(x) ∈ C for μ-almost every x, and hence At ∈ F S. Therefore FS is convex. We now prove that FS is closed in L2(K, μ ; Rd×d). Let An ∈ F S and suppose that An −→ A in

I.58 Proof of Proposition G.11 

Proof. Let U ⊆ RN ×N be an arbitrary nonempty closed convex set, and fix B ∈ RN ×N . We regard RN ×N as a finite-dimensional Hilbert space equipped with the Frobenius inner product 

⟨X, Y ⟩F := tr( X⊤Y ) and the associated norm ∥X∥F = p⟨X, X ⟩F . We first prove directly that the distance from B to U is attained. Define d⋆ := inf A∈U ∥B − A∥F . Because norms are nonnegative, d⋆ ≥ 0. Since U̸ = ∅, choose and fix an arbitrary A0 ∈ U . Then d⋆ ≤ ∥ B − A0∥F < ∞. By the definition of the infimum, for every integer n ≥ 1 there exists An ∈ U such that 

d⋆ ≤ ∥ B − An∥F < d ⋆ + 1

n .

In particular, ∥B − An∥F < d ⋆ + 1 for every n ≥ 1. Using the triangle inequality, 

∥An∥F = ∥An − B + B∥F

≤ ∥ An − B∥F + ∥B∥F

< d ⋆ + 1 + ∥B∥F .

Thus the sequence {An}n≥1 is bounded in the finite-dimensional vector space RN ×N .By finite-dimensional sequential compactness of bounded sets, there exists a subsequence {Anj }j≥1

and a matrix A⋆ ∈ RN ×N such that Anj −→ A⋆ in Frobenius norm . Every member of the subsequence lies in U: Anj ∈ U . Since U is closed, its limit also belongs to U. Hence A⋆ ∈ U .

The Frobenius norm is continuous, so ∥B − Anj ∥F −→ ∥ B − A⋆∥F . On the other hand, by construction, 

d⋆ ≤ ∥ B − Anj ∥F < d ⋆ + 1

nj

.

−→ 0. Therefore ∥B − Anj ∥F −→ d⋆. The limit of a convergent real sequence is unique, so ∥B − A⋆∥F = d⋆. Consequently,

We now verify that each of the geometry-induced families listed in the proposition satisfies the hypotheses of this general projection principle. By Proposition C.7, LS is a closed polyhedral convex cone in Rd×d. In particular, it is closed and convex. Moreover, 0 ∈ L S, so it is nonempty. Therefore every B ∈ Rd×d has a unique Frobenius metric projection onto LS.For the balanced family, Definition E.7 and the constrained-family notation give Lbal  

> S

= LS;1,1.

Proposition C.14, applied with U = 1, V = 1, shows that this set is a closed polyhedral convex cone. Equivalently, it is obtained from LS by adding the linear constraint 1⊤L = 0 . The zero matrix satisfies all of these constraints, so 0 ∈ L bal  

> S

. Hence the balanced generator family is nonempty, closed, and convex and therefore admits a unique metric projection. By Proposition C.18, the block-valued interaction family Ablk  

> S,G

⊆ Rdc ×dc is a finite-dimensional closed polyhedral convex cone. It contains the zero matrix, obtained by setting βrs = 0 for all r, s 

and Di = 0 for every i. Thus Ablk  

> S,G

is nonempty, closed, and convex, and the general projection principle applies in the ambient space Rdc ×dc .The block-valued generator family is 

Lblk  

> S,G

= L ∈ A blk

Lαβ ≥ 0 for α̸ = β, L1dc = 0 .

154 For each pair α̸ = β, the condition Lαβ ≥ 0 defines a closed linear half-space in Rdc ×dc . The condition L1dc = 0 consists of finitely many homogeneous linear equations and therefore defines a closed linear subspace. Consequently, Lblk  

> S,G

is the intersection of the closed convex cone Ablk  

> S,G

with finitely many closed convex sets. Hence it is closed and convex. The zero matrix belongs to this intersection, so the family is also nonempty. Therefore it likewise admits a unique metric projection in Rdc ×dc .This mathematical projection statement is independent of the semantic interpretation of the coordinate-wise order. However, the generator interpretation of Lblk  

> S,G

as a positivity-preserving ordered-feature dynamics is appropriate only in the regime specified in Definition C.19, where the standard positive cone on the flattened feature space has the intended meaning. For generic sign-indefinite hidden fea-tures, the block-valued interaction family remains the natural projection target, whereas the generator terminology should not be imposed merely from the algebraic definition. Finally, Proposition C.26 shows that, when the transfer operators are fixed, Ams , bLms , Lms 

are all finite-dimensional closed polyhedral convex cones in Rd0×d0 . Each contains the zero matrix. Indeed, the zero operator is obtained in Ams by taking every scale-wise interaction equal to zero, and in bLms by taking every scale-wise generator equal to zero. The additional generator constraints defining Lms are also satisfied by the zero matrix. Therefore all three multiscale families are nonempty, closed, and convex. Thus every family listed in the proposition satisfies the hypotheses of the general result proved above in its corresponding finite-dimensional ambient matrix space. Consequently, for every compatible target matrix B, each family admits a unique Frobenius metric projection. 155
